<a name="english"></a>

## English

This reference is for internal reading and for specialists who want to see how the project works inside. It explains why the project is built this way, which options were rejected and what backs each decision. The Russian text is the master, and the translations are updated in the same change. A short description is in the [README](README.md).

### 1. Purpose

Cosmo Game Hub is a static Astro site on GitHub Pages with a collection of classic games.
- **Catalog.** It has eight games. Chess and preferans are playable; checkers, poker, go, monopoly, tic-tac-toe and snake come next.
- **How a game is built.** Each game is a React island; heavy computation runs in a Web Worker.

What the architecture must provide:
- **Many games, one developer.** A game is found, read, changed, moved or removed in one place.
- **Phone, tablet and computer on equal terms.** People play by finger on the way and with a mouse at home — both are tested the same way (Alexey's decision, 09.11.2026). Pages are light, controls never cover each other, and a game is saved after every move, with the «Save» button and when the page is hidden.
- **Fair, strong bots now; online play later.**
  - The bots use the same rules code as the UI and see only what their seat can see.
  - Later the same code will run on a server that referees games between people.
- **Static hosting today.** There is no server yet; everything works from GitHub Pages.

### 2. File tree

```text
cosmo-game-hub/
├── COSMO_GAME_HUB_BIBLE.md       this Bible: five languages in one file, Russian is the master
├── README.md                     the GitHub description in five languages
├── package.json                  workspaces; `npm test` = every test in apps/client/src/**/tests
├── .github/workflows/deploy.yml  builds apps/client and publishes it to GitHub Pages
└── apps/client/
    ├── astro.config.mjs          base '/cosmo-game-hub', React, Tailwind
    ├── tsconfig.json             strict; aliases @shell, @shared, @games
    ├── public/games/chess/       files with a fixed URL: stockfish.js, COPYING.txt, README.md, cover.webp
    ├── public/games/preferans/   cover.svg — the catalog cover (drawn by scripts/preferans-cover.mjs)
    ├── scripts/                  preferans-cover.mjs — the preferans cover; load/ — the load tests (npm run load)
    └── src/
        ├── pages/                routes: index, games, leaderboard, about, games/chess, games/preferans
        ├── shell/                the site around the games
        │   ├── components/       Header, Footer, ScrollToTop, GameCard (Astro components)
        │   ├── layouts/          Layout.astro
        │   ├── styles/           global.css (Tailwind)
        │   ├── data/             mockGames (catalog), mockLeaderboard
        │   ├── utils/            withBase (links under the base URL)
        │   └── tests/            navigation.test.mjs
        ├── shared/               code for two or more games
        │   ├── components/       GameOverOverlay, LanguageSwitch
        │   ├── i18n/             languages.ts, useLanguage.ts — choosing and remembering the language
        │   ├── engine/           random.ts — seeded random numbers
        │   ├── utils/            storageEngine.ts — saves and results
        │   └── tests/            random.test.ts, storage.test.ts, languages.test.ts
        └── games/
            ├── chess/            index.ts · components/ · hooks/ · engine/ · assets/ · tests/ · README.md
            └── preferans/        index.ts · components/ · hooks/ · engine/ (+ ai/) · i18n/ · assets/ · tests/ · README.md · RULES.md
```

### 3. The stack and why

| Layer | Choice | Why | Rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Site | Astro 7, static build | The catalog, leaderboard and About pages are plain HTML; only game pages load JavaScript. GitHub Pages serves static files for free and fast. | A React SPA or Next.js: the whole site would carry React and a router, and server rendering needs a server we do not have. |
| Game islands | React 19 via `client:only="react"` | Chess was already written with React and react-chessboard, and hooks hold game state well. `client:only` mounts the island in the browser only. | `client:load`: Astro would first render the component in Node during the build, where `Worker`, `window` and `localStorage` do not exist. |
| Language | TypeScript, `strict` mode | Game rules carry hundreds of invariants. Types catch mistakes before running, and `noUnusedLocals` catches dead code. | Untyped JavaScript. |
| Shell styles | Tailwind CSS 4 | Simple site pages are quick to lay out with classes. | — |
| Table styles | CSS Modules (`*.module.css`) | Class names are local, so chess and preferans styles never collide. The table layout (seat grid, card sizes as variables, media queries) reads better as CSS. | Tailwind inside games: a table would turn into long class strings. CSS-in-JS: extra code at run time. |
| State | React hooks over a pure engine | The engine is a reducer: `applyAction(state, action)` returns a new state. React only holds the immutable state; all logic is in the engine. | Redux, Zustand: one more layer over something that already is a reducer. |
| Rendering | DOM and CSS | Card and board games are clicks on elements. The DOM gives accessibility (buttons, aria), sharpness on every screen and no image downloads. | Canvas, WebGL and Phaser are for fast animation; for cards and boards they only hurt accessibility. Snake may need a canvas; that will be decided when it is built. |
| Computation | Web Worker | The main thread draws, the worker thinks. | Computing on the main thread: the table would freeze for hundreds of milliseconds. |
| Tests | Node's built-in runner (`node --test`), `.ts` files run directly | No dependencies, and the tests run the real engine files. The price: inside the engine, imports are relative and end in `.ts` (`allowImportingTsExtensions`). | Jest, Vitest: dependencies and configuration for the same result. |
| Randomness | seeded mulberry32 | One number reproduces a deal and the bots' decisions: exact tests, game replays. | `Math.random`: cannot be repeated. For the server, see "Online play". |
| Repository | npm workspaces | A server (`apps/server`) with the same engine will sit next to the client. | — |

### 4. Why folders per game: the options compared

| Option | What it looks like | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Folders by layer** (the tree before 08.11) | `components/`, `hooks/`, `utils/`, `assets/`, each holding a bit of every game | **Rejected.** Chess was spread over 7 places; nine games would make about 60. No folder could be removed without touching another game. |
| **B. Folders per game** | `games/<game>/` holds everything of that game, with familiar layers inside | **Chosen.** One game, one folder, with the usual layers inside: components, hooks, engine. This is the "vertical slice" approach: strong cohesion inside a game, weak coupling between games. |
| **C. A package per game** | `packages/<game>-engine` with its own package.json | **Postponed.** Stronger isolation, but more configuration and no gain today. The CI action builds only `apps/client`, and the engines have exactly one consumer. `engine/` has no React or DOM, so the folder can become a package later without code changes, for example when a game server appears. |

Astro requires routes in `src/pages/`, so the game pages stay there. They are thin: they take the game through its `index.ts` and place it.

### 5. Boundaries: who may import whom

| From → to | Allowed | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `pages` → `games/<game>` (only `index.ts`), `shell` | yes | a route places a game; the game's internals stay closed |
| `games/<game>` → `shared` | yes | shared code is the toolbox of all games |
| `games/<game>` → another game | **no** | build outputs stay separate, and a game can be removed |
| `shared` → `games` or `shell` | **no** | shared code does not depend on its users |
| `games/<game>/engine` → React or DOM | **no** | engines run in workers, in Node tests and later on the server |

- **Aliases.** `@shell/*`, `@shared/*` and `@games/*` match the three top-level parts.
- **Relative imports inside a game.** A game folder can be read and moved whole. Engine code also runs in tests under plain Node, which does not know the aliases.
- **The rule of two.** Code moves to `shared/` only when a second game starts using it. Example: preferans's `PlayingCard` moves there together with poker.

### 6. Bridges between parts

This section shows where one part of the system talks to another, and why the conversation works this way.

1. **Astro page → React island.**
   - A game page is about 10 lines: `<PreferansGame client:only="react" />`.
   - With `client:load`, Astro would first render the component in Node during the build, where `Worker`, `window` and `localStorage` do not exist. `client:only` leaves an empty slot and mounts the island in the browser only.
   - The price: until the JavaScript loads, the table is not visible. For a game this is acceptable.
2. **Table → bots' worker (preferans).**
   - A module worker: `new Worker(new URL('../engine/worker.ts', import.meta.url), { type: 'module' })`. Vite recognises this pattern and builds the worker into a separate hashed file (10 KB).
   - Request `{ id, state, seed, samples, timeBudgetMs }`, response `{ id, action }` or `{ id, error }`.
   - The deal state holds only numbers and arrays. So structured cloning (how `postMessage` copies data) is cheap and loses nothing.
   - Each request gets an increasing `id`, and a response with another `id` is dropped. A late answer for an old position (after "start again" or loading a save) is never applied.
   - One worker serves the whole pool; it is closed (`terminate()`) when the page is left.
   - If the browser has no workers, the same pure function `answerBotRequest` is loaded lazily through `import()` and computes on the main thread.
   - Rejected: `SharedArrayBuffer` (it needs COOP/COEP headers, which GitHub Pages does not set) and Comlink (a dependency for a single kind of message).
3. **Chess → Stockfish over UCI.**
   - Stockfish is written in C++ and compiled to JavaScript (stockfish.js 10.0.2). The file lives in `public/games/chess/`: a worker needs a fixed URL, and the site's `BASE_URL` is added to it.
   - The conversation is text lines of the UCI protocol:
     - `uci`, then `isready` → the engine answers `readyok`;
     - `setoption name Skill Level value N`, `position fen …`, `go depth N` → the engine answers `bestmove e2e4`.
   - `EngineManager` keeps exactly one active search. A new request during a search sends `stop`, and only the latest position stays waiting, so answers for different positions never mix.
   - A worker error calls `destroy()`: every pending wait is closed with an empty move, so nothing hangs.
4. **Game → storage.** Only `shared/utils/storageEngine.ts` knows about `localStorage`. A game passes an id, a schema version and the data (see "Saves").
5. **TypeScript → WebAssembly (future).**
   - A Rust solver will receive the same numbers: three hands as three `u32`, the trump, whose turn it is, and up to two trick cards.
   - No strings or objects cross the boundary. The bridge is a few numbers going into a function and an array of scores coming back, with no serialization.
   - This follows directly from choosing bit masks (see "The preferans engine").
6. **Client → server (future).** Only actions (`Action`) and each seat's view of the deal will travel over the network. These are the same types that travel between the hook and the engine today.

### 7. Performance (measured on the production build, 08.11.2026)

| Page | JavaScript it loads |
|---|---|
| Home, collection, leaderboard, About | no React; only the small "back to top" button script |
| Chess | React 180 KB, 56 KB gzipped (shared: once for the whole site, then from cache) + the island 73 KB (35 KB) |
| Preferans | React (from cache) + the table 50 KB (17 KB); the bots' worker 12 KB (5 KB) as a separate file |
| Shared game-over screen | its own 3 KB file, which every game takes from cache |

- **Per-page splitting.**
  - Astro builds each route separately, and `client:only` islands come alive only on their own page.
  - Stockfish code exists only in the chess file, the solver only in the preferans worker.
  - The worker-less fallback bot (20 KB) loads lazily, only in browsers without workers. Before this change it made the table 17 KB heavier: 44 KB instead of 27.
  - The table menu with its explanations and the score sheet added 23 KB to the table, about 14 KB of it the explanation texts. The menu is the first thing on screen, so moving it into a separate file would gain nothing.
- **Workers.**
  - The preferans bots think in a Web Worker. Whole deals on desktop and on a 390 px phone gave 0 long tasks on the main thread.
  - Chess sends UCI commands to Stockfish in its worker. The reply to e2–e4 came in 0.9 s.
- **Files.**
  - Files imported from `src` get hashed names, so they can be cached for a long time. Small images are inlined into the code.
  - `public/games/<game>/` holds only what needs a fixed URL, such as the Stockfish worker script and its licence.

- **Load tests (08.11.2026, `npm run load`; how they work is in "Checks").**
  - The solver on 400 random full deals: 5 ms median, 47 ms for 95%, 0.33 s at worst (before the numeric position table: 21 ms, 246 ms, 1.8 s). Exactly the same answers.
  - Bots playing whole pools: strong bots 145 deals a minute on one thread and 563 on eight, casual 481 and 1391; 384 deals per strength, no hang. 95% of a strong bot's moves take under 0.1 s; the longest sit at the 0.9 s budget.
  - Stockfish with its time cap: at level 10 a move takes 0.6 s median and 2.0 s at most (5.6 s without the cap). Four engines at once and 60 interrupted searches in a row — every one answered.
  - The table in a browser, 30 deals against strong bots: 95% of bot answers under 91 ms, the longest 894 ms; 0 long tasks on a computer, short ones up to 131 ms on a phone with 4× slower rendering; memory stays flat; 0 errors.
  - The site: 6000 requests from 64 clients — 1517 a second, 95% answered within 74 ms, 0 errors. On a slow phone (CPU ×4, slow 4G) a page starts drawing in 460–888 ms, layout shifts at most 0.052. 20 rounds through the site — memory stays put (3.7 MB).

### 8. Languages and engines: TypeScript, Rust or C++

The decision of 08.11.2026 is a **hybrid**. Rules are written in TypeScript, and the hot compute kernel will move to Rust → WebAssembly. The move happens only when a measurement shows the kernel is limited by speed.

| Part | Language | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Rules, dealing, scoring, card visibility | TypeScript | They are computationally light: checking a move takes microseconds. What matters is a single source of truth. The same code runs in the UI, in the worker, in Node tests and later on the game server (Node). Rules in two languages drift apart sooner or later, and those are the hardest bugs: the UI allows a move that the server rejects. |
| Double-dummy solver, search in checkers and go, odds in poker | TypeScript now, Rust → WASM later | Tight loops with bit operations and hash tables. V8 runs them well. The dense hash table with numeric keys instead of strings was built right in TypeScript (load test of 08.11.2026): a full preferans deal is solved in 5 ms median, 47 ms in 95% of cases and 0.33 s at worst out of 400 deals — 3.5–4 times faster than before, with exactly the same answers. Rust would add running without a garbage collector; the move stays the next step if strong bots ever need many more samples. More samples per move mean stronger bots, especially on misère and early in a deal. |
| Ready strong engines | whatever the authors ship | Stockfish is written in C++ and already compiled to JavaScript (stockfish.js). We do not need our own chess engine. |

**Why Rust and not C++.**
- **Memory safety.** Rust rules out a whole class of memory bugs: out-of-bounds access, use after free. For a kernel that churns through millions of positions this matters.
- **WebAssembly.** Rust has a mature WebAssembly toolchain: the `wasm32-unknown-unknown` target, wasm-bindgen or a plain C ABI.
- **Already mastered.** Alexey already has this pipeline in UNIVERSE: Rust → WASM, an ABI check, tests in CI.
- **The downsides of C++.** Emscripten and manual memory management mean a bigger file and more risk.

**Why not other languages for WebAssembly.**
- **AssemblyScript** looks like TypeScript, but its optimizer is weaker. On this kind of code the gain over V8 is often small.
- **Go (TinyGo) and C# (Blazor)** bring a garbage collector and a runtime into the browser: a bigger file and possible pauses.
- **Zig** is good for WASM, but the language is young and we have no experience with it.

**How the move will work.**
1. The Rust kernel lives in the same worker, behind the same interface (`scoreMoves`).
2. The UI and the rules do not change: only numbers cross the boundary (see "Bridges between parts", item 5).
3. The TypeScript solver stays as the reference: tests compare Rust against it on thousands of positions. It is also the fallback if WASM fails to load.

**First, cheap improvements in TypeScript.**
- The main cost today is the string keys of the transposition table.
- A custom hash table on typed arrays can be written in TypeScript too. 32 cards × 2 bits ("who holds the card") is exactly two 32-bit numbers, plus whose turn it is.
- If that is enough, Rust will not be needed.

**When to move (criterion, a proposal).**
- There is a set of fixed positions by seed. On it we measure milliseconds per full solve and nodes per second, in the browser on a desktop and on a phone.
- We move when both conditions hold:
  - strong bots need more samples per move than fit in the budget (today 8–32 samples in 250–900 ms);
  - Rust is at least 3 times faster, with a WASM file of no more than ~100 KB.

### 9. The preferans engine inside (`games/preferans/engine`)

**Data.**
- A card is a number 0..31: `suit × 8 + rank`. Suits follow the bidding order (♠ ♣ ♦ ♥ = 0..3), ranks run from seven (0) to ace (7).
- A hand, a trick and "cards in play" are 32-bit masks (`CardSet`).
  - Cards of one suit: `hand & (0xff << suit × 8)`.
  - The number of cards is a count of set bits.
- Why masks:
  - legal moves come from a single AND;
  - equivalent cards are found by walking the bits;
  - the state is only numbers, cheap to copy to the worker, to save and to replay;
  - in Rust it is exactly a `u32`.

**Rules as data** (`rules.ts`).
- A `RuleSet` object holds the game prices, whist obligations, whether a ten is checked, half-whist, the all-pass progression, leading from the talon and «здесь».
- Sochi is the `SOCHI` object. Another variant will be another object, not branches in the code.
- `customizeRules(base, options)` applies the table's choices.

**The table menu as data** (`tableOptions.ts`).
- **Required choices:** the number of players, the rule variant, the pool length, the all-pass prices. **Toggles:** the conventions of the variant (on by default) and the extras (off).
- **Conditions on every option:**
  - `requires`: another option must be on first;
  - `conflicts`: the two cannot be on together;
  - `variants` and `players`: only in these variants and with these player counts;
  - `ready`: the engine already plays it.
- **Why an option is closed.** `reasonFor` explains it, always in the same order: not ready yet, variant, player count, conflict, missing requirement. The menu shows that reason in words.
- **Changing the variant or the player count** drops whatever no longer fits, dependents included, and says what was dropped. When two options clash, the one chosen earlier stays.
- **Data from outside.** `normalizeTable` checks a save or received data: unknown values are dropped and closed options removed. The same check will validate a table on the server.
- **Into rules.** `toRuleOptions` turns the choice into engine settings for `customizeRules`.
- **Texts kept apart.** They live in the UI dictionaries (`games/preferans/i18n/`); here there are only ids and conditions, which is why the menu speaks five languages.

**Table rules already in the engine.**
- **Stalingrad.** On a six of spades both defenders must whist: pass and half-whist are unavailable, and the game is always played closed.
- **Concede two or three down.**
  - After the talon, the declarer may refuse to play instead of naming a game.
  - 2 or 3 tricks at the price of the level he bid go into his mountain, and nobody writes whists.
  - The two options exclude each other.

**A deal is a pure state machine** (`deal.ts`).
- Phases: bidding → discard → declaring the game → whist → (whist return) → (how to play: open or closed) → play → done.
- All-pass goes from bidding straight to play; misère skips the declaration and the whist.
- `legalActions(state)` lists the allowed actions. `applyAction(state, action)` checks an action and returns a new state; the old one never changes. The log, replay, undo and position comparison come for free.
- An invalid action throws an error. That is exactly the server-side move check of the future.

**Scoring** (`scoring.ts`). It keeps the pool, mountain and whists for every seat. The final balance in whists always sums to zero, and a test checks it.

**The double-dummy solver** (`ai/solver.ts`). With every hand known, it computes how many tricks a given player takes when everyone plays perfectly.
- **Search.** Alpha-beta with soft bounds (fail-soft). A position's value is the target's number of tricks.
- **Transposition table** at trick boundaries.
  - The key is a string of the three hands and whose lead it is.
  - The value is a lower and an upper bound packed into one number: `lower × 16 + upper` (at most 10 tricks, so 4 bits suffice).
  - Stored bounds narrow the search window at once.
- **Equivalent cards.** Neighbouring cards in one hand are equal when no live card of the opponents and no card of the current trick lies between them. One card per group is searched, and the tree shrinks several times over.
- **Move order.** Leading: high cards first. Following: first the cheapest card that wins the trick, then the low cards. Good ordering gives early cutoffs.
- **Who plays against whom.** Everyone else plays against the target:
  - the defence plays against the declarer;
  - on misère the defence tries to catch the declarer;
  - on all-pass the solver takes a cautious view: "the other two load me".
- **Scoring every move.** `scoreMoves` scores each group of legal cards with a shared table, so the bot gets scores for all moves in one pass.
- **Verification and speed.** On 400 random positions the answer matches a plain full minimax exactly. A full deal is solved in 5 ms median and 0.33 s at worst out of 400 deals. A search can be stopped at a deadline (`scoreMoves(position, deadline)` → `null`), and the bot uses that: after its budget it stops as soon as it has three samples; after three budgets it plays with what it has; if not even the first sample is solved within six budgets, it plays its lowest legal card. So a strong bot's move takes at most about 2.7 s on any device, 5.4 s in the extreme case.
- **Why the key is a string.**
  - Three 32-bit masks make 96 bits, while a JavaScript number holds integers exactly only up to 53 bits.
  - `BigInt` is slow, and a string is the simplest correct key.
  - This is exactly where Rust will gain the most (see "Languages and engines").

**Knowledge and sampling** (`ai/knowledge.ts`).
- Each seat knows its hand, open hands, played cards, its own discard and the open talon on all-pass.
- Whoever failed to follow a suit is void in it. Whoever then also failed to trump is void in trumps too.
- Sampling deals the unknown cards to the hidden hands and to the discard, respecting voids. The most constrained cards are placed first, so sampling rarely hits a dead end.

**The bot** (`ai/bot.ts`).
- **Card play — Perfect-Information Monte Carlo (PIMC).**
  - The bot takes N deals consistent with its knowledge, solves each exactly and plays the card with the best average. On a tie it plays the lowest.
  - At least three deals are solved, then more until the time budget runs out.
  - "Casual" bots: 8 deals and 250 ms; "strong": 32 deals and 900 ms.
- **Bidding, discard, declaration and whist — quick hand estimates** (`ai/evaluate.ts`).
  - What is estimated: the expected tricks with each trump suit, defensive tricks, and the misère danger from the "ladder" of low cards.
  - A single decision needs hundreds of such estimates (66 discards × 5 strains = 330), so a full search does not fit here.
- **Fairness.**
  - The bot receives only what its seat knows. There is no "cheater" level and there never will be.
  - Difficulty is set by thinking time, not by peeking.
  - The study of existing programs showed that players' main complaint is "the bots see my cards". Some apps really do let bots peek.
- **Safety net.**
  - Every bot decision outside card play is checked against `legalActions`. If a heuristic proposes something the table rules do not allow (for example, a new option it does not know yet), the bot takes the first legal action instead of stopping the game.
  - A forced whist under Stalingrad is given without thinking.
  - The bot concedes a hopeless game only when the expected shortfall is at least the allowed concession minus half a trick.
- **Known weaknesses.**
  - The bots bid too cautiously, so all-pass comes up more often than with people. Calibration is listed in "Open tasks".
  - PIMC has two classic flaws:
    - **strategy fusion:** the method assumes it can play differently in each deal, as if it knew the layout;
    - **non-locality:** the method ignores that the opponents' earlier moves hint at their cards.
- **Ways to improve** (from the research).
  - **Exact enumeration instead of sampling.** In open whist and on misère after the cards are shown, only 12 cards are unknown: the hidden hand and the discard. That is just C(12,2) = 66 layouts, and fewer when the talon cards are known. All of them can be solved exactly, with no sampling.
  - **Inference from the auction.** Whoever bid something holds something.
  - **Search over information sets:** αμ, IS-MCTS.
  - **Measuring strength.** Measure strength with duplicate tournaments: the same deals, with seats rotating.

**The worker** (`worker.ts`): see "Bridges between parts", item 2.

### 10. UI and design

- **Room and table.**
  - The cosmic background is made of radial gradients: the stars and the nebula are drawn in CSS, with no images.
  - The card table is deep green felt (`--felt` on `.room`): fibres from `feTurbulence` noise, a faint diagonal twill and the warm light of a lamp above the middle. The walnut rail is a set of `box-shadow` rings (`--rail`). One felt lies under everything on the table: the play area (marked by a thin double line), the action panel and, on a computer, the score sheet. The large sheet window and the menu backdrop are covered with the same felt.
- **Card faces are drawn in CSS** (`PlayingCard.tsx`); **the back is one SVG picture** (`assets/card-back.svg`).
  - Each face has corners with rank and suit and a large suit in the centre. The face needs no file at all: it is sharp at any zoom and pixel density, and the theme is easy to change.
  - The back shows necromancy, black magic and vampires; Alexey asked for it "more evil, harsher": an angry skull with burning eyes and bloody fangs on a blood moon, an inverted pentagram in a necromancer's circle, crossed bones, a red-eyed bat, blood pouring from the top, a black fence in red mist. Card proportions 1 : 1.42; 10 KB (3 KB gzipped).
  - Hearts and diamonds are red, spades and clubs black.
  - When poker arrives, the component moves to `shared/`.
- **Sizes through variables.**
  - Card width is `--card-w: clamp(44px, 6.4vw, 76px)`, and height is ×1.42, the proportion of a real 63 × 88 mm card.
  - On a phone the width is `clamp(38px, 10.5vw, 52px)`.
- **Layout — CSS Grid with named areas.**
  - Desktop: the left opponent, the centre with info and the trick, the right opponent, the hand at the bottom. The score sheet sits on the right, 320 px wide.
  - Screens up to 1023 px: the score sheet moves under the table.
  - Screens up to 640 px: two columns. The opponents are at the top, then the info, the trick and the hand. The whole table fits one phone screen.
- **The score sheet is always in sight, like the paper sheet on a real table** (`Pulka.tsx`, `ScoreStrip.tsx`, `ScoreSheet.tsx`).
  - **Paper and handwriting.** The sheet is ordinary paper, white with a faint touch of cream and fibres (`feTurbulence`); it lies a little crooked and casts a shadow on the felt. The lines are drawn in pencil when the sheet appears. The digits are not a font but pen strokes (`handwriting.ts`: each digit is one path in a 10 × 16 box). A new number is written in front of the player: the stroke is revealed with `stroke-dashoffset` at `pathLength = 1`, one character after another, and the latest number is red. A small repeatable wobble makes the sheet look written, not printed. No font is downloaded.
  - **The drawing.** The pool length sits in a circle in the centre, and every player has a sector. The mountain is next to the centre, then the pool, then the whists at the edge, split between the two opponents.
  - **Entries** are running totals written one after another (2 · 6 · 10), as the scorer does. When a band is full, the oldest entries slide out of sight and the newest is always visible. The pool band fills with colour towards the target.
  - **Fresh entries.** What the last deal wrote flashes once (an underline with `prefers-reduced-motion`).
  - **Computer.** The sheet stays to the right of the table all the time and does not scroll away (`position: sticky`).
  - **Screens narrower than 1024 px.** The sheet does not fit next to the table, so a strip with the score sits above it: for every player the balance in whists, the pool out of the target and the mountain. A tap opens the sheet large — a window on a computer, a sheet sliding up on a phone. The bots wait while the large sheet is open.
  - **Results in the sectors.** Each player's result if the pool closed now sits in his sector by the circle. Players circle the result this way on paper, and the old program «Пуля» did the same.
  - **Layouts for 2, 3 and 4 players**, modelled on Wikipedia, SmartPref, Mail.ru and photos of real sheets.
    - One scheme fits all: a circle in the centre and a sector per player, with mountain → pool → whists inside and the whists split per opponent.
    - Three players: our drawing, with the opponents on the left and right and you at the bottom.
    - Four players: the «envelope» of four trapezoids, with whists on three opponents.
    - Two players: two halves side by side.
    - On paper the numbers are written turned towards each player. On screen we keep them horizontal, which is easier to read, especially on a phone.
  - **Verification.** The history is built by the pure function `buildSheetHistory` from the deal log. A test checks that the last value of every band equals the summed score.
- **The table menu, "like a restaurant"** (`SetupPanel.tsx`), is a modal dialog (`role="dialog"`, `aria-modal`) before a pool.
  - **It looks like a page of a squared school notebook on the felt.** Headings in pen, the chosen value circled by hand (an SVG oval), ticks in drawn boxes, explanations on yellow sticky notes, a closed option in red pen, and "Play" as a red stamp. The printed text is Georgia and the marks use the system's handwriting font, so the menu feels homey and still reads easily.
  - **Order:**
    1. "Play plain preferans" starts at once with the usual rules;
    2. players, rules, pool length, all-pass prices, bots;
    3. the conventions of the variant, then the extras;
    4. "Your table": the order, read back;
    5. "Play".
  - **Incompatible choices.** Whatever contradicts the current choice is disabled, with the reason and what to do next to it: "Cannot be combined with … Switch that off first". Items not ready yet say "soon".
  - **Explanations.** Every item has one in three parts: what it is, how it plays, what it changes in the score.
    - On a computer it pops up on hover. The card lets the pointer through, so the next row stays reachable.
    - The "?" button pins it inside the list — the only way on a touch screen.
- **When a pool starts,** the table scrolls itself into view (`#preferans-room`), so a phone shows the table, not the site header.
- **Accessibility.**
  - A card in the hand is a button with a clear label ("Ace of spades"). The discard selection uses `aria-pressed`, and illegal cards are disabled.
  - Cards on the table have `role="img"` with a label. The table centre has `aria-live="polite"`, so a screen reader announces the played cards.
  - With `prefers-reduced-motion`, animations are turned off.
- **Texts** come in five languages; see "UI and documentation languages". Inside Hebrew text, bids and signed numbers are wrapped in a left-to-right isolate (`ltr()` in `format.ts`); otherwise «6♠» turns into «♠6» and «-40» into «40-».

### 11. Saves and resuming (`shared/utils/storageEngine.ts`)

- **When and where.** A game is saved after every move under the key `cosmo-game-hub:<game>:save`.
- **The prefix.** All sites on `dubnovalexey.github.io` share one browser storage. It also holds `todo-ts:tasks`, `library:books` and other keys, and the prefix keeps them apart.
- **Schema version.**
  - It is stored in every save, so a save from an older version is skipped instead of breaking new code.
  - If the save's shape changes, bump the game's `SAVE_VERSION`. Preferans is at version 2: the settings hold the menu choice (`TableConfig`), checked with `normalizeTable` on load.
- **Results history.** `…:results` keeps the last 20 finished games.
- **No storage.** In private mode, when storage is missing or full, every call is safe: it returns `false` or `null` and throws nothing.
- **Preferans.**
  - It saves the settings, the deal, the score sheet and the log (about 10 KB).
  - Reloading in the middle of a deal brings back the same deal. This was checked on desktop and on a phone.
- **TODO (chess).** Save the PGN and the clocks after every move; chess.js can load a PGN.

### 12. Online play (plan)

- **One engine; the server is the source of truth.**
  - The server runs the same `engine/`.
  - Clients send actions; the server checks them with `applyAction` and sends each seat only its own view of the deal.
  - Hidden cards never leave the server, and the visibility rules already exist (`isHandVisible`).
- **Seats have owners: `human | bot | remote`.** This is already in the preferans hook (`SEAT_KINDS`). A remote seat is the extension point.
- **Two paces.**
  - Real time over WebSocket, with time control and reconnection.
  - Correspondence games with pauses: the server keeps the action log. A game is its seed and its actions, so it can always be replayed exactly.
- **A fair deal.**
  - Today a deal comes from a 32-bit seed (mulberry32). That is enough for bots and tests, but not for ratings: a player who sees their 10 cards can try all 4 billion seeds and learn the other hands.
  - On the server, deals will come from a cryptographic generator.
  - The server publishes a hash of the seed in advance and reveals the seed itself after the deal (a commit–reveal scheme). Everyone can then check that the deal was not rigged.
- **Hosting.** GitHub Pages is static, so a small server is needed: Node with WebSocket, or Cloudflare Durable Objects. That choice is still ahead.
- **Games it covers.**
  - Preferans: 3–4 people, with bots in the empty seats.
  - Poker.
  - Chess in real time and by correspondence.

### 13. UI and documentation languages

- **Five languages in every project:** Russian, Hebrew, English, Spanish and French. This covers both the UI and the documentation (decisions of 08.11.2026).
- **UI (done in preferans on 08.11.2026).**
  - `shared/i18n/languages.ts` picks the language: the saved choice → the browser languages (`iw` counts as Hebrew) → English. The choice is stored under `cosmo-game-hub:language`; `useLanguage()` keeps it in React and tells the page's other islands through a window event. The switch, `shared/components/LanguageSwitch.tsx`, is a plain `select` with each language named in itself.
  - The game's dictionaries live in its folder (`games/preferans/i18n/`). `types.ts` sets the shape and every language fills it in full, so TypeScript reports a forgotten text. The island loads only the chosen language (`import()`, 6–7 KB gzipped) and provides it through context; components read `useTexts()`. Switching the language changes only the texts; the pool goes on.
  - **Grammar.** Sentences about a player take the seat (0 — you, 1 — Orion, 2 — Vega), not a ready name, and each language agrees its verbs itself: «Вы берёте прикуп», «Вега сказала», "You need 6 tricks", «Véga prend le talon», «וגה לוקחת». In Hebrew, instructions use the plural imperative, the usual gender-neutral form of Hebrew interfaces.
  - **Right to left.** The island sets `lang` and `dir` on its root; the table and the score sheet stay `dir="ltr"`, because they are a playing field, not text. Styles use logical properties (`margin-inline-start`, `text-align: start`). Bids and signed numbers are isolated with the invisible LRI/PDI marks.
  - **French typography:** a non-breaking space before ": ; ? !" and inside guillemets; a test keeps it so.
  - **Terms.** Every language shows the Russian word in «» next to the translation («пуля» — pool, pulya, poule, פוליה): players everywhere know the game by its Russian words.
  - **Rules for players:** `games/preferans/RULES.md`, one file in five languages with buttons at the top.
  - The hub shell and chess are still in English; the shell will get its own dictionary.
- **This Bible** is one file at the root, `COSMO_GAME_HUB_BIBLE.md`: five languages one after another, with buttons at the top to jump to a language. Alexey decided so on 08.11.2026: translations are better kept in one file, one after another.
  - Why one file: every language is edited in the same change and they never drift apart, and the number of files does not grow fivefold.
  - The Russian text is the master; the other sections are its translations.
  - The Hebrew text is wrapped in `<div dir="rtl">`, and code blocks and trees in `<div dir="ltr">`. Otherwise a path like `apps/client/` shows up as `/apps/client`.
- **READMEs and game rules** are one file with five sections too.
- **Code comments** are in English and Russian, as the project already does.

### 14. File size and modularity

- **UI and regular code: no more than 800–1000 lines per file.**
  - A longer file is split by responsibility.
  - Today the longest logic file has 276 lines (`useChessLogic.ts`), the longest component 231 lines (`SetupPanel.tsx`), and the longest CSS file 586 lines (`Preferans.module.css`). The dictionaries have about 310 lines per language, but they are data, not logic. The score sheet styles have a file of their own (`ScoreSheet.module.css`).
- **Engines are exempt from this rule** (Alexey's decision of 08.11).
  - An engine is as long as its algorithm needs.
  - Even so, it is split by responsibility: cards, bids, rules, bidding, play, scoring, the deal, the AI parts.
  - The longest engine file has 371 lines (`deal.ts`).

### 15. Checks

- **`npm test` at the root** runs every `apps/client/src/**/tests/*.test.*` with Node's built-in runner: 67 tests, all green (08.11.2026).
  - Node must strip TypeScript types itself: version 23.6 and newer can do it; we use 24.
  - Chess — 7 tests: the engine manager with a Worker mock (the search time cap included), the clocks.
  - Preferans — 49 tests:
    - cards, bids, bidding, the trick;
    - scoring by the rulebook's examples;
    - every path of a deal;
    - the solver against a full minimax, deal sampling;
    - table options;
    - the table menu: conflicts, requirements, changing the variant, checking a save;
    - Stalingrad and conceding, the bots' decisions included;
    - the score sheet history;
    - the five languages: complete dictionaries, agreement with the seat, French typography;
    - time limits: the solver gives up at its deadline, a bot with a tiny budget answers at once with a legal card;
    - bots playing whole deals.
  - Shared code — 9 tests: random numbers, saves, choosing the language.
  - The shell — 2 tests: navigation.
- **Build.** `npm run build --workspace client` produces 6 pages.
- **Types.** `tsc -p apps/client/tsconfig.json` gives 0 errors. It runs through `npx -p typescript@5.9.3`, because TypeScript is not yet a project dependency.
- **Browser.** Headless Chrome went through every page of the site: no errors and no 404 responses.
  - Chess: a move and the engine's reply in 0.9 s.
  - Preferans: whole deals played by clicks, resuming after a reload, a 390 px phone with no horizontal scrolling.
- **Load tests** — `npm run load` (quick version `npm run load:quick`), scripts in `apps/client/scripts/load/`. The browser parts need the production build on `astro preview --port 4324`.
  - `preferans-engine.mjs` — the engine in Node: the solver on 400 random full deals and whole pools of three bots on 1 and 8 threads, with a 20 s watchdog per decision.
  - `chess.mjs` — Stockfish self-play at levels 1–10, 4 engines at once, a "stop storm" (60 interrupted searches), real games by clicks.
  - `preferans-ui.mjs` — deals at the table by clicks against strong bots; every worker request is timed exactly, and on the way the languages are switched and the large sheet is opened.
  - `hub.mjs` — thousands of requests to the server, pages on a computer and on a slow phone (FCP, LCP, CLS, long tasks), 20 rounds through the site to watch memory.
  - The watchdog: a move unanswered for longer than 20–60 s is reported as a hang with its deal seed.
  - Caveat: Chrome's CPU throttling slows the page but not Web Workers, so the engines' speed on a phone is estimated from the Node measurements, and wall-clock limits are what protect a phone.

### 16. How to add a game

1. Create `src/games/<game>/`:
   - `index.ts`, which exports the island;
   - `components/`, `hooks/` and `engine/` (pure logic);
   - `assets/` and `tests/` if needed;
   - `README.md` with the game's rules and how it works.
2. Create `src/pages/games/<game>.astro`, about 10 lines.
3. Mark the game as playable in `src/shell/data/mockGames.ts`.
4. Move shared parts to `shared/` only when a second game uses them.
5. Record the decisions in the Bible, in all five language sections.
6. Run `npm test` and `npm run build --workspace client`.

### 17. Open tasks

- **Preferans:**
  - native speakers proofread the Hebrew, Spanish and French translations;
  - calibrate the bots' bidding and the dealer's decisions (the dealer's whist, shared misère);
  - solve the 66 positions of open whist and misère exactly;
  - backgrounds and frames from the prompts of `DESIGN_CONCEPT_RU.md`, if Alexey generates them;
  - online play.
- **Chess:**
  - a WebAssembly build of Stockfish instead of today's asm.js (Stockfish 10, one thread): several times faster and stronger; settle the GPL license and the file size;
  - save and resume;
  - five UI languages, as in preferans and on the hub's pages;
  - online play in real time and by correspondence.
- **Tooling:**
  - an import-boundary check (ESLint `no-restricted-imports`);
  - `npm test` in CI before publishing;
  - TypeScript in devDependencies.

### 18. Decision log

| Date | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 08.11.2026 | Folders per game (`games/`, `shared/`, `shell/`) instead of folders by layer; chess moved without behaviour changes | Games do not spread across the tree |
| 08.11.2026 | Engines are pure TypeScript inside the game folder, with no separate packages | One consumer; CI builds only `apps/client` |
| 08.11.2026 | Rules in TypeScript, the solver kernel later in Rust → WASM; C++ only in third-party engines | One source of truth; speed where it is needed |
| 08.11.2026 | Cards are numbers 0..31, hands are 32-bit masks | A move in one operation, cheap copies, a WASM bridge with no serialization |
| 08.11.2026 | Bots in a module Web Worker, requests carry an `id`; the fallback loads lazily | The table never freezes, late answers are never applied, the table is 27 KB instead of 44 |
| 08.11.2026 | `client:only` islands | During the build there is no `Worker`, `window` or `localStorage` |
| 08.11.2026 | CSS Modules for the tables, Tailwind for the shell | Local class names; a complex layout reads better in CSS |
| 08.11.2026 | Cards in CSS instead of images | No downloads, sharp on every screen |
| 08.11.2026 | Saving after every move with a schema version; a results history | People play on the way |
| 08.11.2026 | Seat owners `human / bot / remote` | The extension point for online play |
| 08.11.2026 | Bots never peek; difficulty comes from thinking time | Peeking is the main complaint about existing programs |
| 08.11.2026 | mulberry32 only for bots and tests; on the server, a cryptographic generator and commit–reveal | A 32-bit seed can be brute-forced |
| 08.11.2026 | Five languages for the UI and the documentation | An international audience |
| 08.11.2026 | The Bible is one file: five languages one after another, buttons at the top, Russian is the master; `ARCHITECTURE.md` merged into it | Alexey's decision: translations in one file, no extra files; the languages cannot drift apart |
| 08.11.2026 | An 800–1000 line limit for UI and regular code, no limit for engines | Readability |
| 08.11.2026 | The built-in `GameOverOverlay` buttons appear only with a handler | Chess used to show two buttons that did nothing |
| 08.11.2026 | The table menu as data (`tableOptions.ts`): required choices, conventions of the variant, extras; conflicts and requirements with reasons | Like a restaurant: incompatible things cannot be ordered together; the server will need the same checks |
| 08.11.2026 | Every option explains what it is, how it plays and what it changes: on hover and with a "?" button | Phones have no hover; the rules must be visible where people choose |
| 08.11.2026 | The score sheet always in sight: next to the table on a computer, a strip above the table on small screens, large on demand; each player's result in his sector | At a real table the sheet lies in front of everyone; the score is the main result of the game |
| 08.11.2026 | Stalingrad and conceding two or three down in the engine; a bot safety net through `legalActions` | The menu's constraints work for real, not on paper; a new option can never stop the game |
| 08.11.2026 | Deep green felt, one cloth under everything on the table (play area, panel, score sheet, large window, menu backdrop), in a wooden rail | Alexey's request: the sheet "lies on good green felt of a deep green"; a real table has one cloth |
| 08.11.2026 | The score sheet is white paper with a faint touch of cream; lines in pencil, digits as pen strokes written in front of the player | "Only the felt is green; the pool is an ordinary sheet"; the mood of a long evening game where the pool is written by hand |
| 08.11.2026 | The menu is a page of a squared school notebook: the choice circled, ticks, sticky notes, a stamp | Homey and old-school, while the printed text stays easy to read |
| 08.11.2026 | The card back: necromancy, black magic and vampires, one SVG; a second, "more evil" version | Alexey's request |
| 08.11.2026 | Five languages in the game: dictionaries on demand, sentences take the seat, not the name; Hebrew right to left, the table and the sheet left to right | An international audience; without agreement the UI said «Вы берёт прикуп» and «Вега сказал» |
| 08.11.2026 | Rules for players in `RULES.md`, one file for five languages | Like the Bible: translations in one file, no extra files |
| 08.11.2026 | The catalog cover — the score sheet on felt — is drawn by `apps/client/scripts/preferans-cover.mjs` with the same strokes | The cover is regenerated, not redrawn by hand; no fonts and no outside files |
| 08.11.2026 | Every Stockfish search has a time cap: the level's depth or 400 + level × 160 ms, whichever comes first | Load test: at level 10 a move took up to 5.6 s on a fast computer, 20 s and more on a phone |
| 08.11.2026 | The solver's position table is typed arrays with a generation stamp instead of a string-keyed Map; move generation without extra allocations | 3.5–4 times faster with exactly the same answers (checked on 400 full deals); the 16 MB table is created once, only where solving happens |
| 08.11.2026 | Hard wall-clock limits on a bot's move, and a fallback lowest card | A slow device cannot hang on a hard deal; wall-clock limits work on any CPU |
| 08.11.2026 | Load tests in the repository (`apps/client/scripts/load/`, `npm run load`), no packages, over the DevTools protocol | Alexey asked to make sure the engines are fast and never hang; the check can be rerun after every change |
| 09.11.2026 | Two at the table (Gusarik with a dummy) and four (the dealer sits out) — a table layer `engine/table.ts` over the three-seat deal | One deal for every table; the table maps deal seats to players and adds what only the dealer writes |
| 09.11.2026 | Every «coming soon» option plays: whist–pass–half-whist, misère without the talon, dark bids and bombs (Classic), paid talon, American help, the dealer's whist and shared misère at four, teeth | Alexey's request; rules are data (`rules.ts`), every option has a test (`options-extra.test.ts`) |
| 09.11.2026 | New bids are numbered after the classic ones (misère without the talon 26, dark bids 106…110); `bidRank` gives their place in the auction | The numbers of old bids and saved pools never change |
| 09.11.2026 | Bombs live at the table level (the hook); a deal gets them by seat and multiplies the score (`stakesOf`) | Bombs carry over from deal to deal, like the all-pass progression |
| 09.11.2026 | The lair after the concept `DESIGN_CONCEPT_RU.md`: blackened silver, velvet, candelabra, moonlit windows, a quill by the sheet; cards up to 112 px | "The game is the main thing, the rest is atmosphere"; Alexey's brief with the prompt |
| 09.11.2026 | Won tricks lie on the table as separate stacks face down, crosswise, five to a row | Alexey's request: count everyone's tricks at a glance |
| 09.11.2026 | A fan of cards is slots as wide as the step that shrink to the column; the middle of the table catches no taps; no card is lifted over its neighbours | The touch check found the partner's hand under the middle of the table and cards covered by their neighbour |
| 09.11.2026 | Finger and mouse are tested alike: `scripts/load/touch-play.mjs` — CDP touch on phones and tablets, the same scenario with a mouse on a computer, `elementFromPoint` for every button and card | Alexey's decision: "an equal scenario… test them the same way" |
| 09.11.2026 | A «Save» button with the time, and a save when the page hides (`pagehide`, `visibilitychange`); no floating «to top» button on game pages | People play on the way; the «to top» button covered the bids on a phone |
| 09.11.2026 | The hub's pages in five languages: `data-i18n` keys and `shell/i18n`, one language choice shared with the games | The home page was English only |
| 09.11.2026 | 69 characters: 31 new from Alexey's pictures (whole, only scaled down), biographies in five languages; a paid product preview left out | Alexey's request; a picture captioned "instant download" comes with no right to use it |

### 19. The lair, every option and playing by finger (09.11.2026)

- **The design** follows the concept `apps/client/src/games/preferans/DESIGN_CONCEPT_RU.md` (six sections and three Midjourney prompts):
  - emerald felt with an embossed damask and candlelight at the upper corners;
  - cast frames of the characters, plaques, buttons and fields — blackened silver on velvet;
  - the score sheet is a big page with a scorched corner and a raven-feather quill;
  - candelabra, lancet windows with moonlight and bats (`Lair.tsx`) stand at the edges and step away on phones.
- **The screen's main rule:** the cards and the table are the focus, the surroundings only set the mood.
- **Characters.** 69 of them (`characters/roster.ts`): 43 dwellers of the crypt and 26 guests, each with a playing style and a funny biography in five languages.
- **Tables.** Two, three or four players; Sochi, Leningrad, Rostov and Classic rules.
- **Options.** Every one on the menu plays; how, in the menu's explanations and in `RULES.md`, §9.
- **Saves:** after every move, with the «Save» button and when the page is hidden or closed; «Continue» on the menu.
- **Checks.** `npm test` — 90 tests. `scripts/load/touch-play.mjs` plays by finger on phones and tablets and with a mouse on a computer (`INPUT=mouse`) and checks that every button and card can be reached and nothing covers it.

<a name="hebrew"></a>

<div dir="rtl">

## עברית

המדריך הזה נועד לקריאה פנימית ולאנשי מקצוע שרוצים לראות איך הפרויקט עובד מבפנים. הוא מסביר למה הפרויקט בנוי כך, אילו חלופות נדחו ועל מה נשענת כל החלטה. טקסט המקור הוא הרוסי, והתרגומים מתעדכנים באותו שינוי. תיאור קצר נמצא ב-[README](README.md).

### 1. מטרה

הפרויקט Cosmo Game Hub הוא אתר סטטי שבנוי ב-Astro ומתארח ב-GitHub Pages, ובו אוסף של משחקים קלאסיים.
- **הקטלוג.** יש בו שמונה משחקים. שחמט ופרפרנס כבר זמינים למשחק; דמקה, פוקר, גו, מונופול, איקס עיגול ונחש יבואו בהמשך.
- **איך בנוי משחק.** כל משחק הוא "אי" של React, וחישובים כבדים רצים ב-Web Worker.

מה הארכיטקטורה צריכה להבטיח:
- **הרבה משחקים, מפתח אחד.** משחק נמצא, נקרא, משתנה, עובר מקום או נמחק במקום אחד.
- **טלפון, טאבלט ומחשב באותה מידה.** משחקים באצבע בדרכים ובעכבר בבית — שתי הדרכים נבדקות באותו אופן (החלטה של אלכסיי, 09.11.2026). הדפים קלים, הפקדים אף פעם לא מסתירים זה את זה, והמשחק נשמר אחרי כל מהלך, בכפתור «לשמור» וכשהדף מוסתר.
- **בוטים הוגנים וחזקים עכשיו, משחק ברשת בהמשך.**
  - הבוטים משתמשים באותו קוד חוקים כמו הממשק, ורואים רק את מה שנראה מהמקום שלהם.
  - בהמשך אותו קוד ירוץ בשרת שישפוט משחקים בין אנשים.
- **היום אחסון סטטי.** עדיין אין שרת; הכול עובד מ-GitHub Pages.

cosmo-game-hub/

└── apps/client/

    ├── public/games/chess/       קבצים בכתובת קבועה: מנוע השחמט, הרישיון, תמונת השער
    ├── public/games/preferans/   כריכת הפרפרנס בקטלוג (מצייר אותה scripts/preferans-cover.mjs)

        │   ├── i18n/             בחירת השפה וזכירתה
        │   ├── engine/           מספרים אקראיים לפי זרע
        │   ├── utils/            שמירות ותוצאות
